Engineering English

An engineering English curriculum for requirements, design reviews, tradeoffs, testing, failure analysis, quality, safety factors, manufacturability, field issues, and technical disagreement.

For mechanical, electrical, civil, systems, industrial, test, quality, manufacturing, and field engineers, plus engineering managers and technical project leads.

What you will practice · Choose your pace

What you will practice

  • Convert an ambiguous customer request into a measurable proposal while keeping unresolved conditions and approval visible.
  • Challenge a release recommendation by identifying a specific evidence gap and proposing a bounded closure action.
  • Report a failure observation precisely, distinguish mechanism from cause, and propose evidence-led investigation language.
  • Report prototype results with their sample, conditions, criterion, and limits while distinguishing verification from broader validation.
  • Compare design costs on the same volume and cost basis while keeping manufacturability and approval separate from arithmetic.

Choose your pace

Quick practice · 15 minutes: complete one short exchange, check the explanations, and read both roles aloud.

Extended practice · two or three sittings: check the case facts, study the vocabulary and phrases, complete the longer conversation, then rehearse the follow-up exchange.

Level guide: designed for intermediate to advanced learners. B1 learners can start with the short exchanges and supplied phrases; B2 learners can complete the cloze conversations; C1 learners can focus on the distinctions in the language checks. These are teaching suggestions, not a certified level assessment.

Prompts

Original fictional English practice, not engineering design guidance, safety certification, professional approval, or instructions for operating equipment. Numbers and technical cases are invented. Actual design, testing, field action, and compliance decisions require qualified personnel, applicable requirements, approved procedures, and the relevant authority.

01Requirements and ConstraintsLightweight compared with what?+

Your goal

Convert an ambiguous customer request into a measurable proposal while keeping unresolved conditions and approval visible.

This lesson in the book, page 4 ↗

A Read the situation

Lightweight compared with what?

Customer representative Julia asks engineer Wei for a lightweight, durable enclosure. Their meeting proposes a maximum mass of 1.2 kg for the empty enclosure including its cover and fasteners. Electronics are excluded. The proposed service life is five years, but operating temperature, humidity, handling conditions, and the verification method are unresolved. Nothing is approved. Wei must record the proposed mass limit accurately and avoid turning the life target into a demonstrated durability claim.

Julia: Customer representative / Wei: Design engineer

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What is included in the proposed mass limit?

Answer and explanation

B. The empty enclosure, cover, and fasteners

The briefing explicitly includes the enclosure, cover, and fasteners while excluding electronics.

2. What is the status of five years?

Answer and explanation

D. A proposed service-life target

Five years is proposed while conditions and the verification method remain unresolved.

3. Which condition is still open?

Answer and explanation

A. Operating temperature

Operating temperature is listed among the unresolved conditions needed to define the proposal.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Mass is not the same as the mass of the fully populated product. The proposal includes the enclosure, cover, and fasteners but excludes electronics. A five-year target needs defined use conditions and a suitable evidence plan before it can support a durability claim.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
stakeholder need
The capability or outcome a stakeholder wants.capture the stakeholder need
design requirement
A specified condition the design must satisfy.define a design requirement
design constraint
A limit on available design choices.work within a design constraint
mass budget
The planned allocation of allowable mass.allocate the mass budget
maximum mass
The greatest permitted mass under a stated boundary.specify maximum mass
system boundary
The line defining what is included in the system.define the system boundary
operating envelope
The range of conditions for intended operation.define the operating envelope
service life
The intended period of use under specified conditions.state the service life
duty cycle
The pattern of operation and rest over time.specify the duty cycle
environmental condition
A surrounding condition affecting operation or storage.document environmental conditions
acceptance criterion
A condition used to judge whether a result is acceptable.agree an acceptance criterion
verification method
The means used to check a stated requirement.select a verification method
testability
The extent to which a claim can be checked by testing.improve testability
requirement allocation
Assignment of a requirement to a system element.review requirement allocation
derived requirement
A requirement developed from higher-level needs or analysis.justify a derived requirement
design margin
Allowance between a predicted value and a defined limit.preserve design margin
threshold
A stated boundary for an acceptable result.specify the threshold
objective value
A desired value beyond a minimum acceptable level.distinguish threshold and objective
assumption register
A record of unverified premises used in planning or design.update the assumption register
traceability
Recorded connections between needs, requirements, and evidence.maintain traceability
baseline
An approved reference placed under change control.establish a baseline
ambiguity
Wording that permits more than one interpretation.resolve ambiguity
rationale
The reason supporting a requirement or decision.record the rationale
requirement owner
The role responsible for maintaining a requirement.identify the requirement owner

A proposed value is not an approved baseline. Separate what the meeting clarified from what remains open. Terms such as shall, should, and target also need the meaning assigned by the governing specification, not an assumed universal interpretation.

C Notice the language

Ask for the boundary behind the adjective

A request for a robust or lightweight product communicates a preference, not a complete acceptance basis. Ask what is included, under which conditions, and how success will be checked. Keep the discussion focused on the product rather than criticizing the customer for imprecise language.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Clarify the adjective
What maximum mass would count as lightweight?
Set the boundary
Does that value include the cover and fasteners?
Exclude explicitly
Electronics are outside this mass boundary.
State the proposal
We propose a maximum mass of 1.2 kg.
Qualify the status
This is a proposal, not an approved requirement.
Ask about conditions
What operating temperature range must the enclosure withstand?
Clarify the life claim
Five years under which use and maintenance conditions?
Identify a missing input
The handling conditions have not been specified.
Separate target from evidence
The service-life target is not a demonstrated result.
Ask how to check
Which verification method will support acceptance?
Keep the requirement singular
Separate the mass limit from the service-life requirement.
Protect the boundary
A packaging change should not silently alter the measured assembly.
Connect the need
Which customer task makes this limit necessary?
Record a premise
Put the unconfirmed condition in the assumption register.
Request ownership
Who will confirm the environmental requirements?
Close accurately
We have clarified the proposal, but approval is still pending.
6 language and precision notes
Lightweight
Needs a value, boundary, and comparison context.
Durable
Needs relevant conditions and a defined life or performance basis.
Maximum
A limit, not the same as a preferred nominal value.
Including
Identifies items counted in the stated boundary.
Proposed
Keeps a discussion value separate from approval.
Demonstrated
Requires evidence appropriate to the exact claim.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which statement accurately records the mass proposal?

Answer and explanation

D. Maximum 1.2 kg for the empty enclosure including cover and fasteners, excluding electronics

The complete boundary prevents different teams from weighing different assemblies against the same number.

2. Which sentence avoids overstating durability?

Answer and explanation

A. Five years is the proposed service life; operating conditions and verification remain open.

The proposed duration is not evidence of achieved life under unspecified conditions.

3. What should happen to unresolved environmental assumptions?

Answer and explanation

C. Record them with owners for confirmation.

Recording ownership preserves uncertainty and provides a route to resolve the missing inputs.

4. A prototype meets the proposed mass boundary at 1.15 kg. What does that establish about the five-year target?

Answer and explanation

C. Nothing by itself; a mass result does not demonstrate service life under undefined conditions.

Mass and durability are different properties with separate boundaries and evidence needs. The mass comparison does not resolve life, conditions, or approval.

Check explanations in the book, page 85 ↗

E Conversations

Lightweight compared with what?

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Julia: Customer representative / Wei: Design engineer

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • operating envelope
  • traceability
  • rationale
  • system boundary
  • maximum mass
  • requirement owner
  • assumption register
  • baseline
  • verification method
  • service life
  1. Julia
    We need the new enclosure to be lightweight and durable. The previous proposal felt too heavy, but our brief does not yet give a numerical limit.
  2. Wei
    Let us define the 1. Would 1.2 kilograms be the proposed upper limit, and what exactly should be included when we measure it?
  3. Julia
    Use that value for discussion. I mean the empty enclosure with its cover and fasteners, not the electronics that our team will install later.
  4. Wei
    Then the 2 includes those three items and excludes electronics. I will put that beside the number so nobody compares it with a populated assembly.
  5. Julia
    For durability, our commercial team has suggested five years. They have not told us the expected operating temperature or how often the unit will be handled.
  6. Wei
    Five years is a proposed 3, not yet a demonstrated result. We need the use conditions before selecting evidence that could support the requirement.
  7. Julia
    Could we leave the conditions to the design team? I do not want to slow the project by asking the customer for every detail immediately.
  8. Wei
    We can propose an 4, but the customer needs to confirm it. Otherwise we might optimize for conditions that do not match the actual installation.
  9. Julia
    I will ask about temperature, humidity, and handling. Some units may move between workstations, so we should not assume they all stay fixed in one installation.
  10. Wei
    Record each unresolved premise in the 5. Give it an owner and a confirmation point rather than hiding it inside a confident statement about durability.
  11. Julia
    Once the conditions are clear, I assume we will need a way to show that the proposal has been met, not just a convincing description.
  12. Wei
    Yes. The 6 must suit the requirement. We should agree what is checked, on which configuration, and what evidence the acceptance decision will use.
  13. Julia
    I also want the specification to preserve why we chose the limit. A later reviewer may otherwise change it without understanding the installation problem behind it.
  14. Wei
    Keep the 7 with the requirement. That helps reviewers assess whether a proposed change still serves the customer need instead of debating the number in isolation.
  15. Julia
    Let us send the precise mass proposal now and mark the environmental questions as open. I do not want the clear mass limit to make the whole specification look settled.
  16. Wei
    Correct. Clear 8 will connect the need, the agreed wording, and the eventual evidence. It also makes the unresolved links visible during review.
  17. Julia
    I will send the customer the proposed boundary and the missing questions. Please do not label the specification approved until their review is complete.
  18. Wei
    We will establish the 9 only through the agreed approval process. Today's useful result is a precise proposal and an explicit list of remaining decisions.
  19. Julia
    Good. I can explain that progress without claiming the enclosure has already met the five-year target. Who should keep the environmental questions moving?
  20. Wei
    Name a 10 for that follow-up. The record should say who confirms the conditions and when, so the open questions do not disappear between teams.

Answers and explanations
  1. maximum mass Turn 2. Maximum mass states the upper limit, unlike an undefined preference for a lightweight product.
  2. system boundary Turn 4. The system boundary identifies which physical items count toward the proposed mass limit.
  3. service life Turn 6. Service life is the intended period of use under specified conditions, which are still incomplete here.
  4. operating envelope Turn 8. The operating envelope defines the conditions under which the design is expected to operate.
  5. assumption register Turn 10. An assumption register preserves unconfirmed premises and their ownership until they are resolved.
  6. verification method Turn 12. The verification method specifies how evidence will be obtained to check the requirement.
  7. rationale Turn 14. The rationale records why the requirement exists and supports later review of changes.
  8. traceability Turn 16. Traceability links the customer need, requirement, and later evidence without pretending unfinished items are complete.
  9. baseline Turn 18. A baseline is an approved reference, not merely the latest proposal discussed at a meeting.
  10. requirement owner Turn 20. The requirement owner is responsible for maintaining the requirement and coordinating resolution of its open details.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Julia: We need the new enclosure to be lightweight and durable. The previous proposal felt too heavy, but our brief does not yet give a numerical limit.
  2. Wei: Let us define the maximum mass. Would 1.2 kilograms be the proposed upper limit, and what exactly should be included when we measure it?
  3. Julia: Use that value for discussion. I mean the empty enclosure with its cover and fasteners, not the electronics that our team will install later.
  4. Wei: Then the system boundary includes those three items and excludes electronics. I will put that beside the number so nobody compares it with a populated assembly.
  5. Julia: For durability, our commercial team has suggested five years. They have not told us the expected operating temperature or how often the unit will be handled.
  6. Wei: Five years is a proposed service life, not yet a demonstrated result. We need the use conditions before selecting evidence that could support the requirement.
  7. Julia: Could we leave the conditions to the design team? I do not want to slow the project by asking the customer for every detail immediately.
  8. Wei: We can propose an operating envelope, but the customer needs to confirm it. Otherwise we might optimize for conditions that do not match the actual installation.
  9. Julia: I will ask about temperature, humidity, and handling. Some units may move between workstations, so we should not assume they all stay fixed in one installation.
  10. Wei: Record each unresolved premise in the assumption register. Give it an owner and a confirmation point rather than hiding it inside a confident statement about durability.
  11. Julia: Once the conditions are clear, I assume we will need a way to show that the proposal has been met, not just a convincing description.
  12. Wei: Yes. The verification method must suit the requirement. We should agree what is checked, on which configuration, and what evidence the acceptance decision will use.
  13. Julia: I also want the specification to preserve why we chose the limit. A later reviewer may otherwise change it without understanding the installation problem behind it.
  14. Wei: Keep the rationale with the requirement. That helps reviewers assess whether a proposed change still serves the customer need instead of debating the number in isolation.
  15. Julia: Let us send the precise mass proposal now and mark the environmental questions as open. I do not want the clear mass limit to make the whole specification look settled.
  16. Wei: Correct. Clear traceability will connect the need, the agreed wording, and the eventual evidence. It also makes the unresolved links visible during review.
  17. Julia: I will send the customer the proposed boundary and the missing questions. Please do not label the specification approved until their review is complete.
  18. Wei: We will establish the baseline only through the agreed approval process. Today's useful result is a precise proposal and an explicit list of remaining decisions.
  19. Julia: Good. I can explain that progress without claiming the enclosure has already met the five-year target. Who should keep the environmental questions moving?
  20. Wei: Name a requirement owner for that follow-up. The record should say who confirms the conditions and when, so the open questions do not disappear between teams.
Dialogue in the book, page 10 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

What does lightweight mean for this enclosure?

Fictional requirements meeting: a customer requests a lightweight, durable enclosure without numerical limits or operating conditions.

  1. Mechanical engineer: When you say lightweight, do you mean easy to carry or below a specified mass?
  2. Customer engineer: Easy to carry during inspections. We haven't agreed a maximum mass for the complete unit.
  3. Mechanical engineer: Does that include the battery and handle, or only the enclosure itself?
  4. Customer engineer: The complete unit matters to users. My original request wasn't clear about that boundary.
  5. Mechanical engineer: Thanks. What operating conditions and required life should inform the durability requirement?
  6. Customer engineer: Those are still being collected. I cannot give you a verified temperature range today.
  7. Mechanical engineer: Then let's avoid choosing material from an assumed range or treating lightweight as testable yet.
  8. Customer engineer: Could you identify the missing constraints so I can ask the product team focused questions?
  9. Mechanical engineer: Yes: total mass limit, handling conditions, operating environment, and expected service life.
  10. Customer engineer: I'll clarify those before we discuss the weight and durability tradeoff in detail.
A close fit without a defined clearance

Fictional fixture discussion: a drawing calls for a close fit around a removable insert but specifies no clearance.

  1. Design engineer: The drawing says close fit. What must the operator be able to do with the insert?
  2. Manufacturing engineer: Remove it by hand for cleaning, without using a lever or striking the fixture.
  3. Design engineer: Then close fit describes a preference, but we still need a measurable clearance requirement.
  4. Manufacturing engineer: Can we just use the nominal insert dimension and allow no visible movement?
  5. Design engineer: That ignores tolerance, meaning the permitted dimensional variation in the parts we actually receive.
  6. Manufacturing engineer: You're right. We also haven't confirmed the insert supplier's dimensional range for this batch.
  7. Design engineer: Please obtain that range before we set a mating dimension that could prevent removal.
  8. Manufacturing engineer: Should we include the hand-removal requirement in the functional review rather than only the drawing?
  9. Design engineer: Yes. We need both measurable dimensions and a check of the intended handling task.
  10. Manufacturing engineer: I'll bring the supplier information and flag clearance as unresolved until we review it.
Quiet at which operating point?

Fictional fan-cover project: a product brief asks for quiet operation, but speed, measurement distance, and acoustic target are unspecified.

  1. Product engineer: The cover must support quiet operation. Could you clarify the condition behind that requirement?
  2. Product manager: Customers notice noise at their desks, but we haven't specified fan speed or measurement distance.
  3. Product engineer: Those details matter because two tests under different conditions may not be comparable.
  4. Product manager: Could we adopt the competitor's advertised number as our acceptance target for now?
  5. Product engineer: Only after understanding its test conditions. Otherwise the comparison could give us a misleading constraint.
  6. Product manager: Then what can we agree today without pretending we already have a valid acoustic specification?
  7. Product engineer: Agree the intended desk use and assign someone to define a representative operating condition.
  8. Product manager: I'll confirm that use case. The noise target itself will remain an open requirement.
  9. Product engineer: Good. Once we have it, we can assess the airflow and acoustic tradeoff consistently.
  10. Product manager: Bring the missing measurement questions to our next review so we can resolve them explicitly.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Lightweight compared with what?
  1. Read the requirements dialogue. Stress the enclosure, cover, and fasteners included in 1.2 kg, with electronics excluded.
  2. Switch roles. Keep five years as proposed service life while operating conditions, verification, and approval remain open.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

A boundary changes the meaning

A proposal limits an empty housing with lid and screws to 0.8 kg. Batteries are excluded. The operating humidity and acceptance test are not yet agreed. Approval remains pending.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Engineer: "The lid and screws are ___."

Answer and explanation

A. included

The supplied boundary explicitly counts the housing, lid, and screws.

2. Customer: "The batteries are ___."

Answer and explanation

C. excluded

Batteries are explicitly outside the proposed mass boundary in this case.

3. Engineer: "Humidity remains ___."

Answer and explanation

D. unresolved

The briefing states that operating humidity has not yet been agreed.

4. Customer: "This is a proposal, not an approved ___."

Answer and explanation

A. baseline

Approval is pending, so the proposal is not an approved reference.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

REFERENCE
Course: Engineering English
Professional audience: mechanical, electrical, civil, systems, industrial, test, quality, manufacturing, and field engineers, plus engineering managers and technical project leads
Scope: Original fictional English practice, not engineering design guidance, safety certification, professional approval, or instructions for operating equipment. Numbers and technical cases are invented. Lesson 01: Requirements and Constraints
Original fictional case: Customer representative Julia asks engineer Wei for a lightweight, durable enclosure. Their meeting proposes a maximum mass of 1.2 kg for the empty enclosure including its cover and fasteners. Electronics are excluded. The proposed service life is five years, but operating temperature, humidity, handling conditions, and the verification method are unresolved. Nothing is approved. Wei must record the proposed mass limit accurately and avoid turning the life target into a demonstrated durability claim.
Language workshop: Lightweight compared with what?
Communication goal: Convert an ambiguous customer request into a measurable proposal while keeping unresolved conditions and approval visible.
Language guidance: Mass is not the same as the mass of the fully populated product. The proposal includes the enclosure, cover, and fasteners but excludes electronics. A five-year target needs defined use conditions and a suitable evidence plan before it can support a durability claim.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): What maximum mass would count as lightweight? / Does that value include the cover and fasteners? / Electronics are outside this mass boundary. / We propose a maximum mass of 1.2 kg. / This is a proposal, not an approved requirement. / What operating temperature range must the enclosure withstand? / Five years under which use and maintenance conditions? / The handling conditions have not been specified. / The service-life target is not a demonstrated result. / Which verification method will support acceptance? / Separate the mass limit from the service-life requirement. / A packaging change should not silently alter the measured assembly. / Which customer task makes this limit necessary? / Put the unconfirmed condition in the assumption register. / Who will confirm the environmental requirements? / We have clarified the proposal, but approval is still pending.
Vocabulary: stakeholder need: The capability or outcome a stakeholder wants. / design requirement: A specified condition the design must satisfy. / design constraint: A limit on available design choices. / mass budget: The planned allocation of allowable mass. / maximum mass: The greatest permitted mass under a stated boundary. / system boundary: The line defining what is included in the system. / operating envelope: The range of conditions for intended operation. / service life: The intended period of use under specified conditions. / duty cycle: The pattern of operation and rest over time. / environmental condition: A surrounding condition affecting operation or storage. / acceptance criterion: A condition used to judge whether a result is acceptable. / verification method: The means used to check a stated requirement. / testability: The extent to which a claim can be checked by testing. / requirement allocation: Assignment of a requirement to a system element. / derived requirement: A requirement developed from higher-level needs or analysis. / design margin: Allowance between a predicted value and a defined limit. / threshold: A stated boundary for an acceptable result. / objective value: A desired value beyond a minimum acceptable level. / assumption register: A record of unverified premises used in planning or design. / traceability: Recorded connections between needs, requirements, and evidence. / baseline: An approved reference placed under change control. / ambiguity: Wording that permits more than one interpretation. / rationale: The reason supporting a requirement or decision. / requirement owner: The role responsible for maintaining a requirement.
Speaking task: Read the requirements dialogue. Stress the enclosure, cover, and fasteners included in 1.2 kg, with electronics excluded. Switch roles. Keep five years as proposed service life while operating conditions, verification, and approval remain open. Complete and check the transfer. State the 0.8 kg boundary without adding batteries or an approved humidity condition.
Partner: Julia: Customer representative / Wei: Design engineer
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A proposal limits an empty housing with lid and screws to 0.8 kg. Batteries are excluded. The operating humidity and acceptance test are not yet agreed. Approval remains pending.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Julia: I will send the customer the proposed boundary and the missing questions. Please do not label the specification approved until their review is complete.
Wei: We will establish the baseline only through the agreed approval process. Today's useful result is a precise proposal and an explicit list of remaining decisions.
Julia: Good. I can explain that progress without claiming the enclosure has already met the five-year target. Who should keep the environmental questions moving?
Wei: Name a requirement owner for that follow-up. The record should say who confirms the conditions and when, so the open questions do not disappear between teams.
END REFERENCE
Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Lesson 01: Requirements and Constraints
Original fictional case: Customer representative Julia asks engineer Wei for a lightweight, durable enclosure. Their meeting proposes a maximum mass of 1.2 kg for the empty enclosure including its cover and fasteners. Electronics are excluded. The proposed service life is five years, but operating temperature, humidity, handling conditions, and the verification method are unresolved. Nothing is approved. Wei must record the proposed mass limit accurately and avoid turning the life target into a demonstrated durability claim.
Language workshop: Lightweight compared with what?
Communication goal: Convert an ambiguous customer request into a measurable proposal while keeping unresolved conditions and approval visible.
Language guidance: Mass is not the same as the mass of the fully populated product. The proposal includes the enclosure, cover, and fasteners but excludes electronics. A five-year target needs defined use conditions and a suitable evidence plan before it can support a durability claim.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): What maximum mass would count as lightweight? / Does that value include the cover and fasteners? / Electronics are outside this mass boundary. / We propose a maximum mass of 1.2 kg. / This is a proposal, not an approved requirement. / What operating temperature range must the enclosure withstand? / Five years under which use and maintenance conditions? / The handling conditions have not been specified. / The service-life target is not a demonstrated result. / Which verification method will support acceptance? / Separate the mass limit from the service-life requirement. / A packaging change should not silently alter the measured assembly. / Which customer task makes this limit necessary? / Put the unconfirmed condition in the assumption register. / Who will confirm the environmental requirements? / We have clarified the proposal, but approval is still pending.
Vocabulary: stakeholder need: The capability or outcome a stakeholder wants. / design requirement: A specified condition the design must satisfy. / design constraint: A limit on available design choices. / mass budget: The planned allocation of allowable mass. / maximum mass: The greatest permitted mass under a stated boundary. / system boundary: The line defining what is included in the system. / operating envelope: The range of conditions for intended operation. / service life: The intended period of use under specified conditions. / duty cycle: The pattern of operation and rest over time. / environmental condition: A surrounding condition affecting operation or storage. / acceptance criterion: A condition used to judge whether a result is acceptable. / verification method: The means used to check a stated requirement. / testability: The extent to which a claim can be checked by testing. / requirement allocation: Assignment of a requirement to a system element. / derived requirement: A requirement developed from higher-level needs or analysis. / design margin: Allowance between a predicted value and a defined limit. / threshold: A stated boundary for an acceptable result. / objective value: A desired value beyond a minimum acceptable level. / assumption register: A record of unverified premises used in planning or design. / traceability: Recorded connections between needs, requirements, and evidence. / baseline: An approved reference placed under change control. / ambiguity: Wording that permits more than one interpretation. / rationale: The reason supporting a requirement or decision. / requirement owner: The role responsible for maintaining a requirement.
Speaking task: Read the requirements dialogue. Stress the enclosure, cover, and fasteners included in 1.2 kg, with electronics excluded. Switch roles. Keep five years as proposed service life while operating conditions, verification, and approval remain open. Complete and check the transfer. State the 0.8 kg boundary without adding batteries or an approved humidity condition.
Partner: Julia: Customer representative / Wei: Design engineer
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A proposal limits an empty housing with lid and screws to 0.8 kg. Batteries are excluded. The operating humidity and acceptance test are not yet agreed. Approval remains pending.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Julia: I will send the customer the proposed boundary and the missing questions. Please do not label the specification approved until their review is complete.
Wei: We will establish the baseline only through the agreed approval process. Today's useful result is a precise proposal and an explicit list of remaining decisions.
Julia: Good. I can explain that progress without claiming the enclosure has already met the five-year target. Who should keep the environmental questions moving?
Wei: Name a requirement owner for that follow-up. The record should say who confirms the conditions and when, so the open questions do not disappear between teams.
END REFERENCE
Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Lesson 01: Requirements and Constraints

02Design Reviews and Technical PushbackThe drawing fits; the evidence is missing+

Your goal

Challenge a release recommendation by identifying a specific evidence gap and proposing a bounded closure action.

This lesson in the book, page 13 ↗

A Read the situation

The drawing fits; the evidence is missing

At a design review, mechanical lead Ahmed and reviewer Clara examine an enclosure interface. The CAD model shows 2 mm nominal clearance. Requirement R-14 specifies at least 1 mm clearance across the approved dimensional tolerances. The tolerance stack-up has not been documented, and the supplier drawing revision used in the model is unconfirmed. The chair is preparing to recommend release. Clara must distinguish nominal fit from verified minimum clearance and record a specific closure action without claiming the design has already failed.

Ahmed: Mechanical lead / Clara: Design reviewer

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What does the CAD model currently show?

Answer and explanation

C. 2 mm nominal clearance

The model supplies nominal geometry but not the missing tolerance evidence.

2. What does R-14 require?

Answer and explanation

A. At least 1 mm across approved dimensional tolerances

The stated requirement applies across tolerances, not only at nominal dimensions.

3. What is not established?

Answer and explanation

A. Whether the minimum clearance meets R-14

Without the stack-up and confirmed revision, compliance with the minimum remains unverified.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

A nominal 2 mm gap does not establish the minimum gap across permitted variation. Missing evidence also does not prove physical interference. The supported finding is that R-14 is not yet verified on a confirmed configuration.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
design review
A structured examination of a design and its supporting evidence.conduct a design review
review package
The drawings, analyses, and records provided for review.complete the review package
nominal dimension
The stated reference dimension before permitted variation.check the nominal dimension
clearance
The space between adjacent parts.verify minimum clearance
interference
Unwanted overlap or contact between parts.identify potential interference
dimensional tolerance
The permitted variation in a specified dimension.apply dimensional tolerances
tolerance stack-up
The combined dimensional variation affecting an assembly condition.calculate the tolerance stack-up
worst-case analysis
An assessment using the most adverse allowed combination.document a worst-case analysis
datum
A reference used to establish dimensional relationships.identify the datum
mating surface
A surface that joins or interfaces with another part.inspect the mating surface
CAD model
A computer-aided design representation of geometry.update the CAD model
drawing revision
The identified version of an engineering drawing.confirm the drawing revision
assumption log
A record of premises used in an analysis.review the assumption log
open item
An unresolved matter recorded for follow-up.assign an open item
closure evidence
Information demonstrating that an open item is resolved.attach closure evidence
review action
A task assigned as a result of a review.record a review action
technical objection
A reasoned challenge to a technical claim or decision.state a technical objection
dissent
A recorded difference of professional judgment.document unresolved dissent
release recommendation
Advice that a design proceed to a release decision.qualify a release recommendation
release authority
The role authorized to approve the relevant release.identify the release authority
conditional approval
Approval explicitly subject to specified conditions, where allowed.document conditional approval
concurrence
Agreement with a stated position or disposition.obtain reviewer concurrence
design freeze
A defined point after which design changes require controls.prepare for design freeze
action closure
Formal resolution of an assigned review task.verify action closure

Conditional approval is meaningful only if the local process permits it and records its conditions, restrictions, and authority. An informal promise to finish the analysis later must not be written as unconditional release approval.

C Notice the language

Push back on the claim, not the person

A direct technical challenge can be cooperative when it identifies the requirement, the missing evidence, and the decision affected. State what would resolve the concern. Avoid either approving from politeness or treating an incomplete analysis as proof that a colleague designed a defective product.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Locate the concern
My concern is the evidence for R-14.
Acknowledge the work
The nominal geometry is clear in the model.
Distinguish the claim
Nominal clearance is not minimum clearance across tolerances.
Ask for the analysis
Where is the documented tolerance stack-up?
Check the reference
Which supplier drawing revision does the model use?
Avoid alleging failure
I am not saying interference has been demonstrated.
State the gap
We cannot yet verify the required minimum.
Bound the action
Confirm the revision and attach the stack-up for this interface.
Define closure
Closure needs a reviewed result against R-14.
Name responsibility
Ahmed will prepare the analysis for review.
Keep the decision visible
The release recommendation remains unresolved.
Separate meeting roles
Reviewer concurrence is not automatically release authority.
Clarify conditions
What restrictions would apply to conditional approval?
Preserve disagreement
Record the technical objection with its evidence basis.
Prevent silent closure
Do not close the item because the meeting has ended.
Invite resolution
What evidence would let us resolve this concern today?
6 language and precision notes
Fits
May describe nominal geometry without addressing variation.
Minimum
Must be evaluated against the stated range of conditions.
Unverified
Does not mean demonstrated failure.
No objection
Should not conceal an unresolved technical concern.
Conditional
Needs explicit conditions and an authorized process.
Closed
Requires the specified evidence, not merely an assigned task.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which review comment is best supported?

Answer and explanation

D. R-14 remains unverified pending the stack-up and revision confirmation.

This comment identifies the evidence gap without converting uncertainty into failure or compliance.

2. Which closure action addresses both missing inputs for R-14?

Answer and explanation

A. Confirm the supplier drawing revision and review the interface tolerance stack-up against the 1 mm minimum.

The open item concerns combined allowed variation on an identified configuration. Repeating nominal geometry, checking a filename alone, or recording attendance does not resolve it.

3. What may the minutes record now?

Answer and explanation

C. An open verification item and its assigned follow-up

The current record supports a pending item, not an invented approval, failure, or agreement.

4. What would justify closing the item?

Answer and explanation

D. Reviewed evidence for the confirmed configuration against R-14

Closure must resolve the specific requirement and configuration gap identified in the review.

Check explanations in the book, page 87 ↗

E Conversations

The drawing fits; the evidence is missing

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Ahmed: Mechanical lead / Clara: Design reviewer

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • open item
  • release authority
  • technical objection
  • review action
  • Conditional approval
  • drawing revision
  • tolerance stack-up
  • Nominal clearance
  • Action closure
  • closure evidence
  1. Ahmed
    The model shows a two-millimeter gap, and the chair wants to recommend release today. Do you have a specific concern, or can we close the review?
  2. Clara
    My concern is the 1. R-14 applies across the permitted dimensional variation, and the supporting calculation is not in the review package.
  3. Ahmed
    The nominal geometry is comfortably above one millimeter. I agree that it does not show every combination, but I do not want the team to hear that the design has failed.
  4. Clara
    Nor do I. 2 is two millimeters. The minimum remains unverified, which is different from saying that interference has been observed or established.
  5. Ahmed
    That is a fair distinction. The model also came from the supplier file, but I cannot confirm which drawing issue was used without checking our records.
  6. Clara
    Confirm the 3 before completing the calculation. An analysis of an obsolete interface could look convincing while answering the wrong configuration question.
  7. Ahmed
    Please replace check fit with the actual task: confirm the supplier revision and assess minimum clearance against R-14. I can own that analysis.
  8. Clara
    Exactly. Make it a 4 with an owner and a review point. The task is narrow enough to complete without reopening unrelated parts of the design.
  9. Ahmed
    I can own the analysis. I will identify the confirmed supplier revision, state the tolerance assumptions, and present the resulting minimum against R-14.
  10. Clara
    That provides the intended 5. The reviewer still needs to assess the result; attaching a file is not itself the same as resolving the concern.
  11. Ahmed
    Could the chair record conditional approval and allow the release team to continue? I want to avoid delaying work that does not depend on this interface.
  12. Clara
    6 is only available if our process permits it. The record would need clear restrictions and the appropriate decision maker, not a casual promise to finish later.
  13. Ahmed
    I will ask the chair to separate any permitted preparatory work from release. We should not imply that every downstream activity has the same dependency.
  14. Clara
    Agreed. Identify the 7 and let that role decide on the recorded basis. My review comment should not be mistaken for unilateral approval or rejection.
  15. Ahmed
    That is a concern I can answer. Keep the comment tied to the missing stack-up and revision, so the team knows exactly what needs closing.
  16. Clara
    A 8 should be answerable. Here the requirement, missing analysis, and configuration question make the concern concrete enough for us to resolve.
  17. Ahmed
    I will revise the minutes to keep the interface item open. The release recommendation will remain unresolved until the evidence and required review are available.
  18. Clara
    Keep that 9 visible in the action list. A meeting ending or an owner accepting the task must not make its status appear complete.
  19. Ahmed
    Once the reviewed analysis is available, we can record the result and route the recommendation to the authorized decision maker with any remaining conditions.
  20. Clara
    Yes. 10 should identify the evidence and reviewer. That gives the release decision a traceable basis while preserving what the team actually established.

Answers and explanations
  1. tolerance stack-up Turn 2. The tolerance stack-up evaluates combined dimensional variation rather than relying on the nominal CAD gap.
  2. Nominal clearance Turn 4. Nominal clearance describes the reference geometry; it does not establish the smallest gap across allowed variation.
  3. drawing revision Turn 6. The drawing revision identifies the supplier geometry and tolerances that the analysis must actually evaluate.
  4. review action Turn 8. A review action assigns a specific task arising from the meeting, rather than leaving an unowned concern.
  5. closure evidence Turn 10. Closure evidence must show that the specific requirement gap has been resolved on the confirmed configuration.
  6. Conditional approval Turn 12. Conditional approval must follow the applicable process and state its limits; it cannot silently mean unrestricted release.
  7. release authority Turn 14. The release authority is the role permitted to make the release decision, distinct from individual reviewers.
  8. technical objection Turn 16. A technical objection challenges a defined claim with reasons and a route to resolution, not a colleague's competence.
  9. open item Turn 18. An open item records the unresolved matter until the stated closure conditions are satisfied.
  10. Action closure Turn 20. Action closure requires verified resolution of the assigned task, not simply an intention to provide evidence later.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Ahmed: The model shows a two-millimeter gap, and the chair wants to recommend release today. Do you have a specific concern, or can we close the review?
  2. Clara: My concern is the tolerance stack-up. R-14 applies across the permitted dimensional variation, and the supporting calculation is not in the review package.
  3. Ahmed: The nominal geometry is comfortably above one millimeter. I agree that it does not show every combination, but I do not want the team to hear that the design has failed.
  4. Clara: Nor do I. Nominal clearance is two millimeters. The minimum remains unverified, which is different from saying that interference has been observed or established.

Dialogue in the book, page 19 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Approval with an undocumented interface assumption

Fictional design review using the lesson case: approval is being discussed while an interface assumption remains undocumented.

  1. Review chair: The design looks ready. Can we record approval and address minor documentation later?
  2. Systems engineer: My concern is the undocumented interface assumption. We haven't established whether it is minor.
  3. Review chair: Which risk does that create, beyond making the design rationale harder to read?
  4. Systems engineer: If the connected subsystem behaves differently, the verification plan may test the wrong conditions.
  5. Review chair: Are you saying the design is wrong, or that its basis needs confirmation?
  6. Systems engineer: Its basis needs confirmation. I don't have evidence that the design itself is wrong.
  7. Review chair: What practical step would resolve the concern without repeating the whole design review?
  8. Systems engineer: Ask the interface owner to confirm the assumption and identify any affected verification cases.
  9. Review chair: I'll keep that item open and separate it from the parts already reviewed.
  10. Systems engineer: Thank you. Then the decision record will reflect the actual uncertainty rather than implied agreement.
The service opening has no tool-clearance check

Fictional product review: a compact cover design is preferred, but maintenance-tool access has not been checked.

  1. Industrial designer: The smaller cover meets our appearance goal. Can we freeze this geometry today?
  2. Service engineer: I support the compact layout. My concern is that we haven't checked maintenance-tool clearance.
  3. Industrial designer: The fastener is visible in the model. Isn't visibility enough to establish access?
  4. Service engineer: Not necessarily. Seeing a fastener and fitting the intended tool are different checks.
  5. Industrial designer: Do you have a confirmed interference, or is this still a design-review question?
  6. Service engineer: Still a question. I want verification before we claim the service opening is adequate.
  7. Industrial designer: Could we evaluate the tool envelope using the existing model before making another prototype?
  8. Service engineer: That is a useful first step, followed by whatever physical check the review identifies.
  9. Industrial designer: I'll retain the current geometry as proposed and add the access rationale to the review.
  10. Service engineer: I'll provide the intended tool information so we can close the question with evidence.
Removing a test because one prototype passed

Fictional review: one prototype survived a handling demonstration, and a lead proposes removing a planned repeated-handling test.

  1. Project engineer: The prototype survived the demonstration. Could we remove the repeated-handling test to save time?
  2. Reliability engineer: I understand the schedule pressure, but one demonstration doesn't address repeated handling under the test plan.
  3. Project engineer: Would keeping the test imply that the demonstration had no value for the design review?
  4. Reliability engineer: No. It offers evidence for what happened once, not for every condition we planned to verify.
  5. Project engineer: Then can we use that evidence to narrow the test rather than cancel it outright?
  6. Reliability engineer: We can review that proposal against the requirement and document the design rationale for any change.
  7. Project engineer: I don't have authority to alter the agreed verification scope on my own.
  8. Reliability engineer: Let's bring the proposal to the review owner with the evidence and remaining risk clearly stated.
  9. Project engineer: I'll stop describing the test as redundant until that review is complete.
  10. Reliability engineer: Good. We can discuss efficiency without treating an unreviewed reduction as already approved.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

The drawing fits; the evidence is missing
  1. Read the design review. Contrast the 2 mm nominal gap with R-14's minimum of 1 mm across tolerances.
  2. Switch roles. Request the confirmed supplier revision and reviewed stack-up without announcing interference or release approval.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

A nominal fit is not a verified minimum

A model shows 3 mm nominal clearance. The requirement specifies at least 1 mm across tolerances. The stack-up is missing, and the supplier revision is not confirmed.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Reviewer: "Three millimeters is the ___ clearance."

Answer and explanation

D. nominal

The value comes from reference geometry, not a completed variation analysis.

2. Engineer: "The supplier revision needs ___."

Answer and explanation

A. confirmation

The briefing explicitly says the supplier revision has not been confirmed.

3. Reviewer: "The minimum remains ___."

Answer and explanation

C. unverified

A missing stack-up prevents verification of the required minimum across tolerances.

4. Engineer: "The item closes with reviewed ___."

Answer and explanation

C. evidence

Closure needs evidence addressing the requirement, not merely a repeated nominal value.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Course: Engineering English
Professional audience: mechanical, electrical, civil, systems, industrial, test, quality, manufacturing, and field engineers, plus engineering managers and technical project leads
Scope: Original fictional English practice, not engineering design guidance, safety certification, professional approval, or instructions for operating equipment. Numbers and technical cases are invented. Actual design, testing, field action, and compliance decisions require qualified personnel, applicable requirements, approved procedures, and the relevant authority.
Lesson 02: Design Reviews and Technical Pushback
Original fictional case: At a design review, mechanical lead Ahmed and reviewer Clara examine an enclosure interface. The CAD model shows 2 mm nominal clearance. Requirement R-14 specifies at least 1 mm clearance across the approved dimensional tolerances. The tolerance stack-up has not been documented, and the supplier drawing revision used in the model is unconfirmed. The chair is preparing to recommend release. Clara must distinguish nominal fit from verified minimum clearance and record a specific closure action without claiming the design has already failed.
Language workshop: The drawing fits; the evidence is missing
Communication goal: Challenge a release recommendation by identifying a specific evidence gap and proposing a bounded closure action.
Language guidance: A nominal 2 mm gap does not establish the minimum gap across permitted variation. Missing evidence also does not prove physical interference. The supported finding is that R-14 is not yet verified on a confirmed configuration.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): My concern is the evidence for R-14. / The nominal geometry is clear in the model. / Nominal clearance is not minimum clearance across tolerances. / Where is the documented tolerance stack-up? / Which supplier drawing revision does the model use? / I am not saying interference has been demonstrated. / We cannot yet verify the required minimum. / Confirm the revision and attach the stack-up for this interface. / Closure needs a reviewed result against R-14. / Ahmed will prepare the analysis for review. / The release recommendation remains unresolved. / Reviewer concurrence is not automatically release authority. / What restrictions would apply to conditional approval? / Record the technical objection with its evidence basis. / Do not close the item because the meeting has ended. / What evidence would let us resolve this concern today?
Vocabulary: design review: A structured examination of a design and its supporting evidence. / review package: The drawings, analyses, and records provided for review. / nominal dimension: The stated reference dimension before permitted variation. / clearance: The space between adjacent parts. / interference: Unwanted overlap or contact between parts. / dimensional tolerance: The permitted variation in a specified dimension. / tolerance stack-up: The combined dimensional variation affecting an assembly condition. / worst-case analysis: An assessment using the most adverse allowed combination. / datum: A reference used to establish dimensional relationships. / mating surface: A surface that joins or interfaces with another part. / CAD model: A computer-aided design representation of geometry. / drawing revision: The identified version of an engineering drawing. / assumption log: A record of premises used in an analysis. / open item: An unresolved matter recorded for follow-up. / closure evidence: Information demonstrating that an open item is resolved. / review action: A task assigned as a result of a review. / technical objection: A reasoned challenge to a technical claim or decision. / dissent: A recorded difference of professional judgment. / release recommendation: Advice that a design proceed to a release decision. / release authority: The role authorized to approve the relevant release. / conditional approval: Approval explicitly subject to specified conditions, where allowed. / concurrence: Agreement with a stated position or disposition. / design freeze: A defined point after which design changes require controls. / action closure: Formal resolution of an assigned review task.
Speaking task: Read the design review. Contrast the 2 mm nominal gap with R-14's minimum of 1 mm across tolerances. Switch roles. Request the confirmed supplier revision and reviewed stack-up without announcing interference or release approval. Complete and check the transfer. Keep the 3 mm nominal value distinct from the unverified minimum and evidence needed for closure.
Partner: Ahmed: Mechanical lead / Clara: Design reviewer
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A model shows 3 mm nominal clearance. The requirement specifies at least 1 mm across tolerances. The stack-up is missing, and the supplier revision is not confirmed.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Ahmed: I will revise the minutes to keep the interface item open. The release recommendation will remain unresolved until the evidence and required review are available.
Clara: Keep that open item visible in the action list. A meeting ending or an owner accepting the task must not make its status appear complete.
Ahmed: Once the reviewed analysis is available, we can record the result and route the recommendation to the authorized decision maker with any remaining conditions.
Clara: Yes. Action closure should identify the evidence and reviewer. That gives the release decision a traceable basis while preserving what the team actually established.
END REFERENCE
Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.
