Lesson 05: Manufacturability and Cost Engineering
Original fictional case: Design engineer Tessa and manufacturing engineer Arun compare two proposed housings for 3,000 acceptable units. Option A has material cost of $8 and assembly cost of $6 per unit, plus $1,000 one-time tooling. Option B has material cost of $10 and assembly cost of $2 per unit, plus $5,000 tooling. These are the only cost elements in the comparison, with no scrap assumed. Technical suitability, supplier capacity, and approval are unresolved. A slide nevertheless recommends A solely because its material is cheaper.
Language workshop: Cheaper material, more expensive assembly
Communication goal: Compare design costs on the same volume and cost basis while keeping manufacturability and approval separate from arithmetic.
Language guidance: A costs $43,000 and B costs $41,000 for 3,000 units on the stated basis. B saves $2 per unit in recurring cost but requires $4,000 more tooling. Their totals are equal at 2,000 units. These arithmetic results do not establish technical suitability.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Use the same 3,000-unit quantity for both options. / Include material, assembly, and one-time tooling. / Lower material cost does not necessarily mean lower total cost. / A has $14 of material and assembly cost per unit. / B has $12 of recurring cost per unit. / B requires $4,000 more upfront tooling. / The stated totals are $43,000 for A and $41,000 for B. / B is $2,000 lower at this volume. / The total costs are equal at 2,000 units. / The comparison includes only the listed cost elements. / No scrap is assumed in these figures. / A cost advantage does not establish manufacturability. / Can the proposed process deliver the required volume? / B leads on this cost basis, pending the other reviews. / Which production quantity supports that unit-cost figure? / Separate the cost result from the design approval decision.
Vocabulary: manufacturability: How readily a design can be produced by the intended process. / design for assembly: Design choices that simplify or improve assembly, abbreviated DFA. / design for manufacture: Design choices suited to the intended manufacturing process, abbreviated DFM. / bill of materials: The structured list of parts and materials in a product, abbreviated BOM. / material cost: The stated cost of materials used in a unit. / assembly cost: The cost assigned to assembling a unit. / recurring cost: A cost incurred repeatedly as units are produced. / nonrecurring cost: A cost incurred once for the defined program or setup. / tooling investment: Expenditure on equipment or tools needed for production. / unit cost: Cost per unit on a stated inclusion and volume basis. / cost model: A structured calculation connecting cost assumptions and totals. / cost driver: A factor that materially changes cost. / production volume: The number of units made in a stated period or program. / break-even volume: The quantity at which compared total costs are equal. / incremental cost: The change in cost between specified alternatives. / cost trade-off: An exchange between cost categories or other design outcomes. / process capability: The ability of a stable process to meet specified limits. / cycle time: Time taken for one defined production cycle. / takt time: Available production time divided by required customer demand. / scrap allowance: A stated cost or quantity provision for unusable production. / yield assumption: The assumed proportion of production meeting a defined criterion. / tolerance relaxation: Widening a dimensional limit subject to design requirements. / part consolidation: Combining functions to reduce the number of separate parts. / make-or-buy decision: Choosing between internal production and external supply.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10. Correct the $6,000 recurring saving to $2,000 overall after the extra tooling, then check the 2,000-unit crossover. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Separate cost, capacity, process capability, and design approval. Complete and check the transfer. Calculate C at $11,000 and D at $13,000 for 1,000 units, then state the 2,000-unit crossover.
Partner: Tessa: Design engineer / Arun: Manufacturing engineer
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Option C costs $9 per unit plus $2,000 tooling. Option D costs $7 per unit plus $6,000 tooling. Only these costs apply, with no scrap. Compare 1,000 units.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Tessa: We assumed every produced unit is acceptable. I will state that clearly, rather than present the model as though we measured the supplier's losses.
Arun: Keep that yield assumption beside the totals. Scrap or rework could change acceptable-unit cost; the present comparison includes only the three listed cost categories.
Tessa: I will revise the slide: B is two thousand lower at three thousand units on the stated basis, pending suitability, capacity, and approval. No purchase is authorized.
Arun: Also label each unit cost figure as recurring-only or tooling-inclusive. That will stop the next reader comparing twelve dollars with a total-cost figure from another volume.
END REFERENCE

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

06Safety Factors and ComplianceA ratio is not a compliance decision+

Your goal

Question a compliance claim by identifying the applicable requirement, analysis basis, evidence scope, and decision authority.

This lesson in the book, page 49 ↗

A Read the situation

A ratio is not a compliance decision

Engineer Rosa and assurance lead Malik review a fictional design note labeled compliant. A preliminary static model reports a safety factor of 2.0 using its stated capacity-to-demand convention. The applicable requirement, acceptance threshold, and reviewer are not identified. Fatigue and dynamic loading are outside the model. No release approval or formal conformity assessment is recorded. Rosa must correct the note without claiming the design is either safe or unsafe from the ratio alone.

Rosa: Design engineer / Malik: Assurance lead

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What does 2.0 describe in the note?

Answer and explanation

A. A preliminary static-model ratio under its stated convention

The ratio belongs to the preliminary static model and does not establish the broader claims.

2. Which behavior is outside the model's assessment?

Answer and explanation

C. Fatigue and dynamic-loading effects

The briefing explicitly places fatigue and dynamic loading outside the model.

3. What is missing from the compliance claim?

Answer and explanation

A. The applicable requirement and acceptance threshold

A compliance conclusion needs an identified basis, which the note currently does not provide.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

A factor of safety is meaningful only with its stated convention, failure criterion, inputs, and load cases. A value of 2.0 in one preliminary static model is not a universal safety threshold and cannot establish fatigue performance or complete conformity.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
factor of safety
A defined ratio comparing capacity and demand on a stated basis.state the factor of safety
allowable
A permitted value established by the applicable design basis.identify the allowable
design load
A load case specified for design assessment.define the design load
load case
A defined combination of actions considered in an analysis.assess the load case
static analysis
Analysis of conditions treated without significant time-dependent dynamics.review static analysis
dynamic loading
Loading whose time variation requires dynamic effects to be considered.assess dynamic loading
fatigue
Progressive damage associated with repeated loading.evaluate fatigue
ultimate capacity
Capacity associated with a defined ultimate failure limit.state ultimate capacity
yield limit
A boundary associated with the onset of specified permanent deformation.identify the yield limit
buckling
Instability involving a change in structural form under loading.assess buckling
failure criterion
The condition used to define failure in an analysis.specify the failure criterion
load combination
A defined set of loads considered together.document the load combination
model validity
The degree to which a model is suitable for its intended assessment.assess model validity
analysis limitation
A condition or behavior not adequately addressed by an analysis.state analysis limitations
applicable requirement
A requirement that governs the specific product or situation.identify applicable requirements
compliance matrix
A record linking requirements to evidence and status.maintain a compliance matrix
conformity assessment
Evaluation of whether specified requirements are fulfilled.identify the conformity assessment route
normative reference
A referenced document needed to apply a specification's requirements.check normative references
standard edition
The identified version of a published standard.confirm the standard edition
evidence package
The records supporting a technical or compliance conclusion.assemble the evidence package
independent review
Review by a suitably separate qualified reviewer.arrange independent review
authorized sign-off
Recorded approval by a person with the relevant authority.obtain authorized sign-off
deviation approval
Authorized acceptance of a defined departure where permitted.record deviation approval
release restriction
A stated limit on what may proceed under a decision.communicate release restrictions

Requirements may come from law, contracts, standards, and internal specifications, with different authority and scope. Identify what actually applies. Neither a missing compliance record nor a favorable ratio alone justifies declaring the product categorically safe or unsafe.

C Notice the language

Ask what compliant refers to

Compliance is a relationship to specific applicable requirements, not a general compliment about design quality. Ask for the requirement, version, evidence, limitations, and authorized decision. A numerical margin may be useful evidence, but its meaning depends on the model and the failure modes considered.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Ask for the basis
Compliant with which applicable requirement?
Identify the version
Which standard edition does the assessment use?
Name the convention
The reported factor uses the stated capacity-to-demand ratio.
Limit the result
This is a preliminary static-model result.
Ask for the criterion
Which failure criterion and acceptance threshold apply?
Expose excluded behavior
Fatigue and dynamic loading are outside this model.
Avoid a universal rule
A factor of 2.0 is not a universal acceptance threshold.
Separate analysis from approval
A calculated result is not authorized sign-off.
Request traceability
Link each requirement to its supporting evidence.
Check model scope
Does the model represent the load cases being claimed?
Keep status precise
The compliance conclusion is not yet established.
Avoid unsupported alarm
The missing record does not itself prove physical failure.
Ask about review
Who is qualified and authorized to review this assessment?
Clarify a departure
Any permitted deviation needs the required approval.
Preserve restrictions
State what cannot proceed while the item remains open.
Close the note
Report the result, limits, missing basis, and pending decision.
6 language and precision notes
Safe
A broad claim that cannot rest on one unexplained ratio.
Compliant
Requires an identified applicable requirement and evidence.
Factor
Specify the ratio convention and relevant failure criterion.
Preliminary
Signals a maturity limit, not a release authorization.
Outside scope
Makes excluded behavior visible to the reader.
Signed
Verify the signer's authority and the exact decision covered.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which replacement note is justified?

Answer and explanation

D. The preliminary static model reports 2.0; applicable acceptance requirements and review remain unresolved.

The replacement preserves the numerical result and clearly identifies the missing decision basis.

2. Why is the applicable requirement necessary?

Answer and explanation

A. It establishes what must be assessed and the acceptance basis.

Without the governing requirement, the ratio cannot be judged against the correct acceptance conditions.

3. The static factor is 2.0 and fatigue is outside the model. Which conclusion is justified?

Answer and explanation

B. Fatigue performance is not established by this model; it needs an appropriate assessment basis.

A static capacity-to-demand ratio is not a fatigue-life result. An excluded behavior is unassessed here, not automatically passed, failed, or irrelevant.

4. What should a compliance matrix connect?

Answer and explanation

C. Applicable requirements, evidence, and assessment status

The matrix supports traceability between the actual requirements, supporting records, and current conclusions.

A ratio is not a compliance decision

Rosa: Design engineer / Malik: Assurance lead

  16. Malik

  18. Malik

Answers and explanations
  1. applicable requirement Turn 2. The applicable requirement defines the governing obligation and acceptance basis missing from the current note.
  2. factor of safety Turn 4. The factor of safety is a defined ratio in this model, not a universal measure proving complete product safety.
  3. failure criterion Turn 6. The failure criterion defines the condition against which capacity and demand are assessed.
  4. analysis limitations Turn 8. Analysis limitations identify excluded behaviors, including fatigue and dynamic loading, that the reported model cannot establish.
  5. model validity Turn 10. Model validity concerns whether the model is appropriate for the claimed assessment, beyond simply producing a numerical output.
  6. standard edition Turn 12. The standard edition identifies the specific version whose requirements are being applied, rather than assuming all versions are interchangeable.
  7. compliance matrix Turn 14. A compliance matrix connects each applicable requirement with evidence and its assessment status.
  8. conformity assessment Turn 16. Conformity assessment evaluates specified requirements through the applicable route; writing an internal note does not establish its completion.
  9. authorized sign-off Turn 18. Authorized sign-off is an actual approval by the relevant authority, which is not supplied by the preliminary calculation.
  10. evidence package Turn 20. The evidence package must support the exact claim through identified requirements, suitable analyses, limitations, and review records.
Read the complete conversation

Dialogue in the book, page 55 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Which requirement supports compliant?

Fictional engineering review using the lesson case: a note says compliant but identifies neither an applicable requirement nor supporting review evidence.

  1. Review coordinator: The note calls the design compliant. Could you identify the requirement and evidence behind that statement?
  2. Design engineer: I copied the wording from an earlier review, but I haven't checked its applicability here.
  3. Review coordinator: Please locate the relevant requirement and review record before tomorrow's discussion, or flag what remains missing.
  4. Design engineer: Would a calculation showing a positive margin be enough to keep the compliance wording?
  5. Review coordinator: A calculation may support a particular check, but it doesn't establish every applicable requirement was reviewed.
  6. Design engineer: Understood. I'll distinguish the calculated margin from the broader code-compliance claim in the note.
  7. Review coordinator: Also ask the qualified reviewer to confirm which requirements apply; neither of us should guess.
  8. Design engineer: Should I mark compliance as pending while that applicability review is incomplete?
  9. Review coordinator: Yes. State the evidence gap accurately rather than asserting either compliance or noncompliance without support.
  10. Design engineer: I'll bring the identified records and unresolved questions to the reviewer through our established process.
A safety factor without its load basis

Fictional internal calculation review: a summary lists a safety factor of two but omits the load case and calculation revision.

  1. Engineering manager: The summary gives a safety factor of two. Can we approve the component from that number?
  2. Calculation reviewer: I need the load case and calculation revision before interpreting what that factor represents.
  3. Engineering manager: Could you explain why the ratio alone isn't enough for the review audience?
  4. Calculation reviewer: A margin only has meaning relative to its stated assumptions, inputs, and applicable acceptance basis.
  5. Engineering manager: Then please request the source calculation rather than suggesting the factor itself is definitely inadequate.
  6. Calculation reviewer: Agreed. We have missing context, not evidence yet that the design meets or misses the requirement.
  7. Engineering manager: Can the calculation owner provide the load basis before our scheduled design checkpoint?
  8. Calculation reviewer: I'll ask for that timing and request an explicit gap update if the records aren't ready.
  9. Engineering manager: Keep the decision pending and route the adequacy question to the responsible qualified engineer.
  10. Calculation reviewer: I will. The summary will identify the factor as reported, with interpretation awaiting its basis.
A supplier certificate names another revision

Fictional equipment review: a supplier certificate names revision B, while the supplied assembly is labeled revision C; equivalence has not been established.

  1. Procurement engineer: We have the supplier certificate. Can the review team close the compliance evidence item?
  2. Quality engineer: The certificate names revision B, but the delivered assembly is revision C. We need clarification.
  3. Procurement engineer: Could that simply be a labeling difference with no technical effect on the product?
  4. Quality engineer: Possibly, but equivalence isn't established. Please ask the supplier which configuration their evidence actually covers.
  5. Procurement engineer: Should I request a corrected certificate or a documented explanation of the revision relationship?
  6. Quality engineer: Request clarification and supporting records first; we shouldn't prescribe a correction before understanding the discrepancy.
  7. Procurement engineer: I'll ask for a response before the acceptance meeting and flag any delay to you.
  8. Quality engineer: Thank you. Keep the original certificate with its revision information rather than replacing the record silently.
  9. Procurement engineer: I'll describe the mismatch without claiming the assembly is unsafe or fully compliant.
  10. Quality engineer: Then the responsible reviewer can assess applicability using evidence instead of an unsupported revision assumption.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

A ratio is not a compliance decision
  1. Read the assurance discussion. Keep the reported 2.0 factor within its preliminary static-model basis.
  2. Switch roles. Name the unidentified requirement and excluded fatigue and dynamic effects without declaring universal safety or failure.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Keep the model inside its scope

A preliminary static report gives a factor of 1.8 under its stated convention. It excludes fatigue, has no identified acceptance threshold, and has not received release approval.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Engineer: "The reported value belongs to the ___ model."

Answer and explanation

C. static

The briefing identifies the report as a preliminary static assessment.

2. Reviewer: "The model excludes ___."

Answer and explanation

C. fatigue

Fatigue is explicitly outside the scope of the supplied model.

3. Engineer: "The acceptance threshold remains ___."

Answer and explanation

C. unidentified

No applicable acceptance threshold is supplied in the briefing.

4. Reviewer: "Release approval has not been ___."

Answer and explanation

A. granted

The briefing states that the report has not received release approval.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

REFERENCE
Course: Engineering English
Professional audience: mechanical, electrical, civil, systems, industrial, test, quality, manufacturing, and field engineers, plus engineering managers and technical project leads
Scope: Original fictional English practice, not engineering design guidance, safety certification, professional approval, or instructions for operating equipment. Numbers and technical cases are invented. Actual design, testing, field action, and compliance decisions require qualified personnel, applicable requirements, approved procedures, and the relevant authority.
Lesson 06: Safety Factors and Compliance
Original fictional case: Engineer Rosa and assurance lead Malik review a fictional design note labeled compliant. A preliminary static model reports a safety factor of 2.0 using its stated capacity-to-demand convention. The applicable requirement, acceptance threshold, and reviewer are not identified. Fatigue and dynamic loading are outside the model. No release approval or formal conformity assessment is recorded. Rosa must correct the note without claiming the design is either safe or unsafe from the ratio alone.
Language workshop: A ratio is not a compliance decision
Communication goal: Question a compliance claim by identifying the applicable requirement, analysis basis, evidence scope, and decision authority.
Language guidance: A factor of safety is meaningful only with its stated convention, failure criterion, inputs, and load cases. A value of 2.0 in one preliminary static model is not a universal safety threshold and cannot establish fatigue performance or complete conformity.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Compliant with which applicable requirement? / Which standard edition does the assessment use? / The reported factor uses the stated capacity-to-demand ratio. / This is a preliminary static-model result. / Which failure criterion and acceptance threshold apply? / Fatigue and dynamic loading are outside this model. / A factor of 2.0 is not a universal acceptance threshold. / A calculated result is not authorized sign-off. / Link each requirement to its supporting evidence. / Does the model represent the load cases being claimed? / The compliance conclusion is not yet established. / The missing record does not itself prove physical failure. / Who is qualified and authorized to review this assessment? / Any permitted deviation needs the required approval. / State what cannot proceed while the item remains open. / Report the result, limits, missing basis, and pending decision.
Vocabulary: factor of safety: A defined ratio comparing capacity and demand on a stated basis. / allowable: A permitted value established by the applicable design basis. / design load: A load case specified for design assessment. / load case: A defined combination of actions considered in an analysis. / static analysis: Analysis of conditions treated without significant time-dependent dynamics. / dynamic loading: Loading whose time variation requires dynamic effects to be considered. / fatigue: Progressive damage associated with repeated loading. / ultimate capacity: Capacity associated with a defined ultimate failure limit. / yield limit: A boundary associated with the onset of specified permanent deformation. / buckling: Instability involving a change in structural form under loading. / failure criterion: The condition used to define failure in an analysis. / load combination: A defined set of loads considered together. / model validity: The degree to which a model is suitable for its intended assessment. / analysis limitation: A condition or behavior not adequately addressed by an analysis. / applicable requirement: A requirement that governs the specific product or situation. / compliance matrix: A record linking requirements to evidence and status. / conformity assessment: Evaluation of whether specified requirements are fulfilled. / normative reference: A referenced document needed to apply a specification's requirements. / standard edition: The identified version of a published standard. / evidence package: The records supporting a technical or compliance conclusion. / independent review: Review by a suitably separate qualified reviewer. / authorized sign-off: Recorded approval by a person with the relevant authority. / deviation approval: Authorized acceptance of a defined departure where permitted. / release restriction: A stated limit on what may proceed under a decision.
Speaking task: Read the assurance discussion. Keep the reported 2.0 factor within its preliminary static-model basis. Switch roles. Name the unidentified requirement and excluded fatigue and dynamic effects without declaring universal safety or failure. Complete and check the transfer. Read the 1.8 factor, missing threshold, and absent release approval as separate facts.
Partner: Rosa: Design engineer / Malik: Assurance lead
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A preliminary static report gives a factor of 1.8 under its stated convention. It excludes fatigue, has no identified acceptance threshold, and has not received release approval.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Rosa: Then the revised note can preserve the factor of two, identify the model exclusions, and say that the compliance conclusion remains unestablished pending the required review.
Malik: Also distinguish that from authorized sign-off. No release approval is recorded, and a technical result must not silently become permission to proceed.
Rosa: I will keep the current restrictions visible and identify who will confirm the requirements and review the evidence. I will not call the design either safe or unsafe from this ratio alone.
Malik: Good. The evidence package should let a qualified decision maker see what is supported and what is missing. That is more useful than an unsupported compliance label.
END REFERENCE

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

07Field Issues and Customer CommunicationCorrect the cause without losing the customer+

Your goal

Correct an unsupported causal statement, acknowledge customer impact, and give a useful next update without promising an unconfirmed resolution.

This lesson in the book, page 58 ↗

A Read the situation

Correct the cause without losing the customer

A customer reports two output interruptions from field unit L18. Service engineer Marco previously emailed that humidity caused the interruptions, although humidity remains only a hypothesis and no root cause is confirmed. Customer engineer Nia requests a correction and a resolution date. The investigation is open; records are being gathered, no repair or operating change is authorized, and Marco can commit only to an update by 10 a.m. on October 2. The cases are fictional and supply no instruction to continue operating the unit.

Nia: Customer engineer / Marco: Service engineer

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What needs correction?

Answer and explanation

A. Humidity was stated as a confirmed cause without confirmation.

The earlier email converted an unconfirmed humidity hypothesis into a causal finding.

2. What can Marco commit to?

Answer and explanation

A. An update by 10 a.m. on October 2

The only supplied commitment is the next update, not resolution or operating authorization.

3. Which statement preserves the customer's report?

Answer and explanation

D. Unit L18 has two reported output interruptions.

The two reported events remain relevant even though their cause is unresolved.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Reported interruptions are not erased by uncertainty about cause. The error is the email saying humidity caused them. The correction should explicitly withdraw that unsupported conclusion while preserving humidity as a hypothesis and the customer report as relevant evidence.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
field report
Information about product behavior in actual use.assess a field report
customer impact
The effect of an issue on the customer's work or results.acknowledge customer impact
reported symptom
Behavior described by a user but not necessarily independently verified.record the reported symptom
confirmed finding
A conclusion supported by the completed relevant checks.separate confirmed findings
working hypothesis
A provisional explanation guiding investigation.qualify the working hypothesis
causal statement
Wording that says one condition produced another.correct a causal statement
correction notice
A message explicitly amending earlier information.issue a correction notice
case reference
An identifier connecting records for the same issue.use the case reference
unit serial number
A unique identifier for an individual product unit.confirm the unit serial number
installed configuration
The hardware, software, and settings present in the field.document the installed configuration
operating history
Records of how and when a unit has been used.request operating history
evidence request
A specific request for information needed in an investigation.make an evidence request
traceable record
A record connected to its source, time, and relevant item.retain a traceable record
return authorization
Permission and arrangements for returning an item.confirm return authorization
chain of custody
A record of possession and transfer of evidence or an item.maintain chain of custody
service instruction
An authorized direction for servicing or handling a product.follow the service instruction
field action
A coordinated response affecting products in use.assess a field action
impact statement
A bounded description of an issue's effects.prepare an impact statement
interim update
A progress report before the final outcome is available.provide an interim update
resolution commitment
A promise about when or how an issue will be resolved.avoid an unsupported resolution commitment
update cadence
The agreed frequency or timing of progress communication.agree an update cadence
escalation contact
The named route for raising concerns requiring further attention.identify the escalation contact
case owner
The person responsible for coordinating a case.name the case owner
closure confirmation
A recorded check that the case's closure conditions are met.obtain closure confirmation

An update commitment is not a repair deadline. State what the update will contain, even if the investigation remains open. Do not improvise operating or repair instructions; any such direction must come through the authorized process for the actual product and risk.

C Notice the language

Correct the record explicitly

A quiet change of wording may leave the customer relying on the earlier claim. Identify what was overstated, give the corrected status, and acknowledge the practical effect of uncertainty. A promised update is valuable when it specifies what will be reported, even if resolution timing remains unknown.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Acknowledge the correction
Our earlier email overstated the cause.
Withdraw the unsupported claim
Humidity has not been confirmed as the cause.
Preserve the hypothesis
It remains one explanation under investigation.
Recognize the report
We have recorded two interruptions on unit L18.
Acknowledge the impact
I understand that the uncertainty affects your planning.
Ask for specific records
Please connect each event to its time and installed configuration.
Avoid false reassurance
We do not yet have evidence to rule out recurrence.
Separate the commitments
I can commit to an update, not a resolution date.
State the next contact
I will update you by 10 a.m. on October 2.
Define update contents
The update will distinguish findings, open questions, and next steps.
Keep authority clear
No repair or operating change is authorized by this message.
Route service decisions
Product-specific instructions must come through the authorized service process.
Connect the correction
Attach this correction to the same case record.
Name ownership
I remain the case owner for communication.
Avoid premature closure
Sending the correction does not close the investigation.
Close the loop
Please confirm that the corrected status reached the relevant team.
6 language and precision notes
Reported
Attributes a statement without dismissing it.
Caused
Requires stronger evidence than a plausible association.
Correction
Explicitly identifies the earlier information that changed.
Update
A communication milestone, not necessarily a technical resolution.
No evidence yet
Does not mean proof of absence.
Closed
Needs the case's actual closure conditions to be satisfied.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which opening best corrects the earlier message?

Answer and explanation

B. Our email overstated the cause; humidity remains unconfirmed.

The opening explicitly corrects the unsupported certainty without discarding the reported events.

2. Which deadline statement is supported?

Answer and explanation

A. I will send an investigation update by 10 a.m. on October 2.

Only the communication deadline is authorized by the supplied facts.

3. Which request could help distinguish causes without presuming humidity is responsible?

Answer and explanation

B. Provide event times, installed configuration, and available environmental records linked to L18.

Traceable observations let investigators test competing explanations. Filtering or labeling the evidence to fit the prior claim would prejudge the investigation.

4. What should the correction not imply?

Answer and explanation

B. Permission to change operation or perform an unapproved repair

The briefing gives no authorized operating or repair change, so the message must not create one.

Check explanations in the book, page 97 ↗

E Conversations

Correct the cause without losing the customer

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Nia: Customer engineer / Marco: Service engineer

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • traceable record
  • interim update
  • resolution commitment
  • working hypothesis
  • case owner
  • Closure confirmation
  • field report
  • service instruction
  • correction notice
  • causal statement
  1. Nia
    Your email said humidity caused the two interruptions on L18. Our team now understands that the cause has not been confirmed, so we need the record corrected.
  2. Marco
    You are right. The earlier 1 was too strong. Humidity is unconfirmed, and I will explicitly correct the message rather than quietly change the wording in our next update.
  3. Nia
    Please keep the two interruptions in the record. Uncertainty about the explanation does not change what our operators reported or the disruption they experienced.
  4. Marco
    We will preserve the 2. The correction concerns our explanation, not whether your team raised the events. Both reports remain part of the investigation for this unit.
  5. Nia
    Should our internal summary remove humidity entirely? I want it to be accurate without hiding an explanation that your engineers are still examining.
  6. Marco
    Describe it as a 3. It is one explanation being investigated, not a confirmed finding and not something we have ruled out through completed checks.
  7. Nia
    Please identify the earlier email in the correction. People are still forwarding it, so they need an explicit statement that the cause has not been confirmed.
  8. Marco
    I will issue a 4 on the same case. It will say that our previous message overstated the cause and that the investigation remains open.
  9. Nia
    We can supply the event times and the installed version information. Tell us how to connect those records so the engineers can distinguish the two occurrences.
  10. Marco
    Each 5 should identify L18, the event time, and the installed configuration. We will coordinate the evidence request through the case rather than ask you to guess which material matters.
  11. Nia
    Planning needs a date. What can you commit to now, and what is still preventing a resolution estimate?
  12. Marco
    I cannot give a 6 yet. I can commit to an update by ten a.m. on October second, and I understand that uncertainty creates a planning burden for you.
  13. Nia
    What will that update contain if the cause is still open? Saying we are working on it would give us little new information.
  14. Marco
    The 7 will separate confirmed findings, open hypotheses, outstanding evidence, and the next review point. It will not turn the communication deadline into a promised repair date.
  15. Nia
    One colleague asked whether the correction means we should change how the unit is used or attempt a repair. We have not received an authorized instruction.
  16. Marco
    This message is not a 8. Any operating or repair direction must come through the authorized product-specific process. I will route that question to the responsible team rather than improvise an answer.
  17. Nia
    Please clarify ownership. We have heard from several people, and we need to know who will collect the questions and respond.
  18. Marco
    I will remain the 9 for communication. Your questions will stay linked to the same record, and I will identify the appropriate escalation contact if additional attention is needed.
  19. Nia
    That gives us a usable correction and a clear next contact. I will circulate the amended status, but I will not mark the technical issue resolved.
  20. Marco
    Correct. 10 comes only when the relevant conditions are met. Today's correction restores an accurate record while the investigation and technical decisions continue.

Answers and explanations
  1. causal statement Turn 2. The causal statement claimed humidity produced the interruptions, which the available investigation has not established.
  2. field report Turn 4. The field report records the customer's two interruptions and remains relevant while the cause is unresolved.
  3. working hypothesis Turn 6. A working hypothesis remains under investigation without being presented as a supported causal finding.
  4. correction notice Turn 8. A correction notice explicitly amends the earlier claim so recipients do not continue relying on its unsupported certainty.
  5. traceable record Turn 10. A traceable record connects evidence to its source, event time, unit, and configuration rather than leaving context ambiguous.
  6. resolution commitment Turn 12. A resolution commitment promises an outcome or timing that remains unsupported while the cause and repair are unresolved.
  7. interim update Turn 14. An interim update reports progress before final resolution and should distinguish findings, uncertainties, and next steps.
  8. service instruction Turn 16. A service instruction must come through the authorized product-specific process; the correction does not authorize operating or repair changes.
  9. case owner Turn 18. The case owner coordinates the investigation communication and follow-up so the customer has a clear responsible contact.
  10. Closure confirmation Turn 20. Closure confirmation requires the actual case conditions to be satisfied; correcting an email alone does not resolve the underlying issue.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Nia: Your email said humidity caused the two interruptions on L18. Our team now understands that the cause has not been confirmed, so we need the record corrected.
  2. Marco: You are right. The earlier causal statement was too strong. Humidity is unconfirmed, and I will explicitly correct the message rather than quietly change the wording in our next update.
  3. Nia: Please keep the two interruptions in the record. Uncertainty about the explanation does not change what our operators reported or the disruption they experienced.
  4. Marco: We will preserve the field report. The correction concerns our explanation, not whether your team raised the events. Both reports remain part of the investigation for this unit.
  5. Nia: Should our internal summary remove humidity entirely? I want it to be accurate without hiding an explanation that your engineers are still examining.
  6. Marco: Describe it as a working hypothesis. It is one explanation being investigated, not a confirmed finding and not something we have ruled out through completed checks.
  7. Nia: Please identify the earlier email in the correction. People are still forwarding it, so they need an explicit statement that the cause has not been confirmed.
  8. Marco: I will issue a correction notice on the same case. It will say that our previous message overstated the cause and that the investigation remains open.
  9. Nia: We can supply the event times and the installed version information. Tell us how to connect those records so the engineers can distinguish the two occurrences.
  10. Marco: Each traceable record should identify L18, the event time, and the installed configuration. We will coordinate the evidence request through the case rather than ask you to guess which material matters.
  11. Nia: Planning needs a date. What can you commit to now, and what is still preventing a resolution estimate?
  12. Marco: I cannot give a resolution commitment yet. I can commit to an update by ten a.m. on October second, and I understand that uncertainty creates a planning burden for you.
  13. Nia: What will that update contain if the cause is still open? Saying we are working on it would give us little new information.
  14. Marco: The interim update will separate confirmed findings, open hypotheses, outstanding evidence, and the next review point. It will not turn the communication deadline into a promised repair date.
  15. Nia: One colleague asked whether the correction means we should change how the unit is used or attempt a repair. We have not received an authorized instruction.
  16. Marco: This message is not a service instruction. Any operating or repair direction must come through the authorized product-specific process. I will route that question to the responsible team rather than improvise an answer.
  17. Nia: Please clarify ownership. We have heard from several people, and we need to know who will collect the questions and respond.
  18. Marco: I will remain the case owner for communication. Your questions will stay linked to the same record, and I will identify the appropriate escalation contact if additional attention is needed.
  19. Nia: That gives us a usable correction and a clear next contact. I will circulate the amended status, but I will not mark the technical issue resolved.
  20. Marco: Correct. Closure confirmation comes only when the relevant conditions are met. Today's correction restores an accurate record while the investigation and technical decisions continue.
Dialogue in the book, page 64 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Correcting an unconfirmed field-failure cause

Fictional customer update using the lesson case: a previous message presented an unconfirmed field-failure cause as fact; investigation remains open.

  1. Customer engineer: Your update said installation caused the field failure. Has that actually been established?
  2. Service engineer: No. Our wording was too definite, and I apologize for presenting an unconfirmed explanation as fact.
  3. Customer engineer: Then what can we rely on from that message when briefing our operations team?
  4. Service engineer: The failure report is recorded, but the cause remains under investigation. Installation conditions are one open question.
  5. Customer engineer: Does this correction also mean you are confirming that the failure qualifies for warranty coverage?
  6. Service engineer: No. I cannot determine coverage here; that question needs the appropriate review alongside the investigation.
  7. Customer engineer: Please keep those two issues separate so the correction doesn't create another unsupported conclusion.
  8. Service engineer: I will. We'll provide a factual investigation update and route the coverage question to its owner.
  9. Customer engineer: And don't call a proposed corrective action a proven fix before the evidence supports it.
  10. Service engineer: Agreed. The corrected message will distinguish observations, hypotheses, proposed actions, and decisions still pending.
A replacement shipment is not proof of a fix

Fictional service case: a replacement display has shipped after a field failure, but the cause and corrective-action effectiveness are unconfirmed.

  1. Customer coordinator: Your message said permanently resolved because a replacement display shipped. Is the root cause confirmed?
  2. Service lead: It isn't. That wording overstated what the shipment establishes, and I should correct it.
  3. Customer coordinator: Please explain the distinction so our staff know what has actually changed today.
  4. Service lead: A replacement has shipped to support recovery. We haven't confirmed the cause or a permanent corrective action.
  5. Customer coordinator: Will you need information about the original unit's installation conditions before the investigation continues?
  6. Service lead: The investigation owner will specify the needed records. We shouldn't ask you to guess or alter equipment.
  7. Customer coordinator: Can you give us a useful update without guaranteeing that the replacement cannot have the same issue?
  8. Service lead: Yes. I'll confirm shipment status, state the investigation is open, and name the next contact.
  9. Customer coordinator: That is more accurate. We'll keep the original failure record available through the agreed channel.
  10. Service lead: Thank you. I'll replace the earlier closure language and keep recovery separate from verified prevention.
No fault found does not erase the report

Fictional returned-unit investigation: a display worked during a bench check, but the customer's intermittent field symptom has not been reproduced.

  1. Customer engineer: Your report says no fault exists. We experienced an intermittent blank display twice last week.
  2. Test engineer: You're right to challenge that wording. Our bench check found no fault under the conditions tested.
  3. Customer engineer: So you aren't saying our field report is false or that the issue is closed?
  4. Test engineer: Correct. We haven't reproduced the symptom, which is different from proving it never occurred.
  5. Customer engineer: What should the corrected message say about installation conditions and the next investigation step?
  6. Test engineer: It should request the relevant recorded conditions through the service process, without asserting they caused the issue.
  7. Customer engineer: Please don't ask our operators to recreate a potentially disruptive failure on active equipment.
  8. Test engineer: Agreed. We'll review existing records and have the investigation owner define any appropriate controlled checks.
  9. Customer engineer: I'll share the timestamps from our reports and keep the description limited to what staff observed.
  10. Test engineer: I'll correct the report and clearly mark the intermittent field issue as still under investigation.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Correct the cause without losing the customer
  1. Read the customer exchange. Explicitly correct the earlier humidity claim while retaining the two reported L18 interruptions.
  2. Switch roles. Promise the October 2, 10 a.m. update without creating a repair deadline or operating instruction.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

An update is not a repair promise

A supplier wrongly called contamination the confirmed cause. It is still a hypothesis. The investigation is open. The supplier promises a progress report Friday at noon, not a repair.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Supplier: "Our earlier claim needs a ___."

Answer and explanation

C. correction

The earlier message presented an unconfirmed explanation as an established cause.

2. Customer: "Contamination remains a ___."

Answer and explanation

D. hypothesis

The briefing explicitly says contamination has not been confirmed as the cause.

3. Supplier: "Friday noon is the next ___."

Answer and explanation

A. update

The promised milestone is a progress report rather than a completed repair.

4. Customer: "The investigation remains ___."

Answer and explanation

C. open

Neither the cause nor the resolution has been established in the supplied facts.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

REFERENCE
Course: Engineering English
Professional audience: mechanical, electrical, civil, systems, industrial, test, quality, manufacturing, and field engineers, plus engineering managers and technical project leads
Scope: Original fictional English practice, not engineering design guidance, safety certification, professional approval, or instructions for operating equipment. Numbers and technical cases are invented. Actual design, testing, field action, and compliance decisions require qualified personnel, applicable requirements, approved procedures, and the relevant authority.
Lesson 07: Field Issues and Customer Communication
Original fictional case: A customer reports two output interruptions from field unit L18. Service engineer Marco previously emailed that humidity caused the interruptions, although humidity remains only a hypothesis and no root cause is confirmed. Customer engineer Nia requests a correction and a resolution date. The investigation is open; records are being gathered, no repair or operating change is authorized, and Marco can commit only to an update by 10 a.m. on October 2. The cases are fictional and supply no instruction to continue operating the unit.
Language workshop: Correct the cause without losing the customer
Communication goal: Correct an unsupported causal statement, acknowledge customer impact, and give a useful next update without promising an unconfirmed resolution.
Language guidance: Reported interruptions are not erased by uncertainty about cause. The error is the email saying humidity caused them. The correction should explicitly withdraw that unsupported conclusion while preserving humidity as a hypothesis and the customer report as relevant evidence.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Our earlier email overstated the cause. / Humidity has not been confirmed as the cause. / It remains one explanation under investigation. / We have recorded two interruptions on unit L18. / I understand that the uncertainty affects your planning. / Please connect each event to its time and installed configuration. / We do not yet have evidence to rule out recurrence. / I can commit to an update, not a resolution date. / I will update you by 10 a.m. on October 2. / The update will distinguish findings, open questions, and next steps. / No repair or operating change is authorized by this message. / Product-specific instructions must come through the authorized service process. / Attach this correction to the same case record. / I remain the case owner for communication. / Sending the correction does not close the investigation. / Please confirm that the corrected status reached the relevant team.
Vocabulary: field report: Information about product behavior in actual use. / customer impact: The effect of an issue on the customer's work or results. / reported symptom: Behavior described by a user but not necessarily independently verified. / confirmed finding: A conclusion supported by the completed relevant checks. / working hypothesis: A provisional explanation guiding investigation. / causal statement: Wording that says one condition produced another. / correction notice: A message explicitly amending earlier information. / case reference: An identifier connecting records for the same issue. / unit serial number: A unique identifier for an individual product unit. / installed configuration: The hardware, software, and settings present in the field. / operating history: Records of how and when a unit has been used. / evidence request: A specific request for information needed in an investigation. / traceable record: A record connected to its source, time, and relevant item. / return authorization: Permission and arrangements for returning an item. / chain of custody: A record of possession and transfer of evidence or an item. / service instruction: An authorized direction for servicing or handling a product. / field action: A coordinated response affecting products in use. / impact statement: A bounded description of an issue's effects. / interim update: A progress report before the final outcome is available. / resolution commitment: A promise about when or how an issue will be resolved. / update cadence: The agreed frequency or timing of progress communication. / escalation contact: The named route for raising concerns requiring further attention. / case owner: The person responsible for coordinating a case. / closure confirmation: A recorded check that the case's closure conditions are met.
Speaking task: Read the customer exchange. Explicitly correct the earlier humidity claim while retaining the two reported L18 interruptions. Switch roles. Promise the October 2, 10 a.m. update without creating a repair deadline or operating instruction. Complete and check the transfer. Withdraw confirmed contamination and retain a hypothesis, Friday-noon update, and open investigation.
Partner: Nia: Customer engineer / Marco: Service engineer
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A supplier wrongly called contamination the confirmed cause. It is still a hypothesis. The investigation is open. The supplier promises a progress report Friday at noon, not a repair.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Nia: Please clarify ownership. We have heard from several people, and we need to know who will collect the questions and respond.
Marco: I will remain the case owner for communication. Your questions will stay linked to the same record, and I will identify the appropriate escalation contact if additional attention is needed.
Nia: That gives us a usable correction and a clear next contact. I will circulate the amended status, but I will not mark the technical issue resolved.
Marco: Correct. Closure confirmation comes only when the relevant conditions are met. Today's correction restores an accurate record while the investigation and technical decisions continue.
END REFERENCE
Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.
